L1966: Source: Applicant's study report
L1967: One subject (Subject (b) (6)) was discontinued after completing Period 1 due to a positive cotinine test at admission to Period 2. The inclusion criteria required that enrolled subjects be non-smokers and/or had not used nicotine or nicotine-containing products for at least 6 months; or have discontinued smoking or the use of nicotine/nicotine-containing products for at least 3 months.
L1968: **Reviewer's Comment:** Dolutegravir is metabolized primarily by UGT1A1 with some contribution from CYP3A and is also a substrate of UGT1A3, UGT1A9, BCRP, and P-gp *in vitro*. Based on *in vitro* data and results of a clinical trial with midazolam, MK-1439 is not expected to be an inhibitor of UGT1A1, CYP3A, or P-gp. *In vitro*, MK-1439 inhibits BCRP with an IC50 value of 51 µM, well above the plasma Cmax value of MK-1439 (&lt;4 µM), and thus inhibition of BCRP at the systemic level is not anticipated. However, there is potential for inhibition of BCRP at the gut level ([I2]/IC50 &gt; 10, where [I2] is defined as the dose of MK-1439 on a molar basis/250 mL), which may be the cause of the increases in dolutegravir exposure observed in this trial. However, the impact on dolutegravir is not considered clinically meaningful. Of note, MK-1439 dose used in this study is 2-fold higher than the proposed to-be-marketed dose (100 mg).
L1969: ## **Conclusions:**
L1970: 1. The steady-state AUC0-24hr, Cmax and C24hr of dolutegravir were increased by 36%, 43%, and 27%, respectively, when coadministered with multiple doses of MK-1439. The magnitude of the effect is not considered clinically significant.
L1971: 2. Steady-state AUC0-24hr, Cmax and C24hr of MK-1439 are not significantly altered by coadministration with multiple doses of dolutegravir.
L1972: ## **Protocol P050: Drug-Drug Interaction Study of MK-1439 (Doravirine) with MK-8742 + MK-5172 (Elbasvir + Grazoprevir)**
L1973: **Study Design:** This was a nonrandomized, fixed-sequence, single-site, open-label, 3-period trial of doravirine (MK-1439), elbasvir (MK-8742) and grazoprevir (MK-5172) in 12 healthy male and female adult subjects 19 to 55 years of age.
L1974: Period 1: Doravirine alone	100 mg doravirine (1 x 100 mg) administered QD on Days 1 to 5, N=12 Subjects
Period 2: Elbasvir + grazoprevir co-administered	50 mg elbasvir (1 x 50 mg) and 200 mg grazoprevir (2 x 100 mg) co-administered QD on Days 1 to 10, N=12 Subjects
Period 3: Doravirine + elbasvir + grazoprevir co-administered	100 mg doravirine (1 x 100 mg), 50 mg elbasvir (1 x 50 mg), and 200 mg grazoprevir (2 x 100 mg) co-administered, QD on Days 1 to 5, N=12 Subjects
L1975: Period 2 commenced after a washout period of at least 5 days (120 hours) from the last dose in Period 1. There was no washout following Period 2. Day 1 of Period 3 immediately followed Day 10 of Period 2.

‡ Pseudo Within-Subject %CV=100 \times \sqrt{(\sigma_a^2 + \sigma_b^2 - 2\sigma_{ab})/2}, where \sigma_a^2 and \sigma_b^2 are the estimated variances on the log scale for the two treatment groups, and \sigma_{ab} is the corresponding estimated covariance, each obtained from the linear mixed effects model.
* Median (min, max) reported for Tmax.
GM: Geometric least-squares mean, GMR: Geometric least-squares mean ratio, CI: Confidence interval.
L1991: Source: Applicant's study report
L1992: **Reviewer's Comment:** The mechanism of the increase in doravirine concentrations may be through inhibition of CYP3A in the gut likely by grazoprevir. Grazoprevir is a weak CYP3A inhibitor, while data suggest that elbasvir is not a CYP3A inhibitor (IC_{50} &gt; 100 \mu M). In a DDI study of grazoprevir with midazolam, a sensitive CYP3A substrate, grazoprevir increased the AUC0-inf of midazolam by approximately 30%, which is comparable to the effect of grazoprevir on doravirine. This magnitude of the DDI is not considered clinically significant.
L1993: ## **Conclusion:**
L1994: - Co-administration of multiple once daily 100 mg doses of doravirine with multiple once daily doses of 50 mg elbasvir + 200 mg grazoprevir resulted in modest increases in doravirine AUC, Cmax and C24 values of 56%, 41% and 61%, respectively. However, these increases are not considered clinically relevant.
- Co-administration of multiple once daily doses of 50 mg elbasvir + 200 mg grazoprevir with multiple once daily doses of 100 mg doravirine results in similar elbasvir and grazoprevir exposure compared to that after a single oral dose of 50 mg elbasvir + 200 mg grazoprevir alone.
L1995: ## **Protocol P045: A Multiple-Dose Clinical Trial to Study the Effect of MK-1439 (doravirine) on Methadone Pharmacokinetics**
L1996: **Study Design:** Fourteen (14) male and female subjects 18 to 65 years of age who remained on stable oral methadone maintenance therapy 20 – 200 mg (20-180 mg actual) once daily (QD) were enrolled. Subjects received their usual oral maintenance dose of methadone on Days 1 through 7. On Days 2 through 6, subjects received an oral dose of doravirine 100 mg QD immediately after methadone administration.

L2013: Source: Applicant's study report
L2014: **Reviewer's Comment:** While methadone is not known to induce CYP metabolism, the modest reduction (AUC 26%↓ and Cmax 24% ↓) in doravirine exposure may be related to decreased gut motility which may potentially affect absorption. It has been reported that methadone decreases exposure of didanosine (AUC0-inf reduced by 17% and Cmax reduced by 16% from VIDEK EC package insert; and AUC0-12 reduced by 57% and Cmax reduced by 66% from publication by P.M. Raniney *et al.*, Journal of Acquired Immune Deficiency Syndromes, 2000) and stavudine (AUC0-12 reduced by 23% and Cmax reduced by 44% (P.M. Raniney *et al.*, Journal of Acquired Immune Deficiency Syndromes, 2000)) which may be the result of delayed gastric emptying. However, based on Phase 2 data which demonstrated efficacy at doses as low as 25 mg, this reduction in doravirine exposure is unlikely to be clinically meaningful.
L2015: ## **Conclusion:**
L2016: - Multiple-dose administration of 100 mg of doravirine QD to subjects on stable methadone regimens does not affect the PK of methadone.
- Multiple dose administration of methadone reduced doravirine AUC and Cmax by 26% and 24%, respectively, when compared to the historical data. However, the effect is not considered clinically significant.
L2017: ## **Protocol P053: A Randomized, Open-Label, 3-Period Crossover Study to Evaluate the Pharmacokinetic Interaction of MK-1439 (Doravirine) and Ledipasvir/Sofosbuvir in Healthy Adult Subjects**
L2018: **Study Design:** This was an open-label, randomized, 3-period, crossover trial to evaluate the PK interaction of DOR and ledipasvir/sofosbuvir in 14 healthy adult subjects. On Day 1 of each period, subjects received a single-oral dose of either DOR alone, ledipasvir/sofosbuvir alone, or DOR co-administered with ledipasvir/sofosbuvir in a randomized manner. There was a washout interval of 14 days between each dose.

L2021: Source: the applicant's study report

Plasma samples for sofosbuvir and GS-331007 analysis were collected from each subject at predose and at selected time points over 120 hours following each administration of sofosbuvir. Plasma samples for doravirine analysis were collected from each subject at predose and at selected time points over 72 hours following each administration of doravirine.

L2045: ## Conclusion:
L2046: - Coadministration of a single oral dose of 90 mg ledipasvir/400 mg sofosbuvir and a single oral dose of 100 mg doravirine results in similar ledipasvir, sofosbuvir, and GS-331007 exposure compared to that after a single oral dose of 90 mg ledipasvir/400 mg sofosbuvir alone.
- Coadministration of a single oral dose of 90 mg ledipasvir/400 mg sofosbuvir with a single oral dose of 100 mg doravirine has a small but not clinically meaningful effect on doravirine PK. Doravirine AUC0-∞, Cmax, and C24 increased by 15%, 11%, and 24% following coadministration compared to doravirine alone.
L2047: ## Protocol P048: A Study to Evaluate the Effect of Multiple Doses of MK-1439 (Doravirine) on Metformin Pharmacokinetics in Healthy Subjects
L2048: **Study Design:** This was an open-label, two-period, two-treatment, fixed-sequence PK drug interaction study to evaluate the effect of multiple doses of doravirine (MK-1439) on the single-dose PK of metformin in 14 healthy male and female subjects. Subjects received Treatment A in Period 1 and Treatment B in Period 2.
L2049: Treatment A consisted of a single oral dose of a 1000 mg tablet of metformin [immediate release (IR)]. Treatment B consisted of a once daily oral dose of 100 mg doravirine from Day 1 to Day 7 and single oral dose of a 1000 mg metformin on Day 5. There was a washout period of at least 3
L2050: ## NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation – NDA 210806 and NDA 210807
## Doravirine (DOR) and DOR/3TC/DOR
L2051: days between metformin drug administration in Period 1 and the first doravirine drug administration in Period 2.
L2052: A standardized breakfast was given within 25 minutes prior to metformin drug administration. On Days 1-4 and Days 6-7, a light breakfast meal was given approximately 30 minutes prior to doravirine administration.

L2068: **Reviewer's Comment:** The oral absorption, hepatic uptake and tubular uptake of metformin are mediated largely by organic cation transporters (OCT-3, OCT-1, and OCT-2) and multidrug and toxin extrusion-1 and -2K (MATE-1/2K) transporters are thought to be involved in the tubular secretion of metformin. Doravirine has been studied *in vitro* as an inhibitor of OCT-2, and MATE-1/2K and showed it is not likely to be an inhibitor for OCT-2 or MATE-1/2K; The study result is consistent with the *in vitro* studies. In addition, it indicates that doravirine is not likely to affect substrates of OCT-1 or OCT-3.
L2069: **Conclusion:** Administration of MK-1439 100 mg tablets (doravirine) once daily does not have a clinically meaningful effect on the pharmacokinetics of metformin.

L2097: **Reviewer's Comment:** Doravirine has poor, pH-independent aqueous solubility (solubility remains poor across pH) and high permeability. The chemical structure of doravirine does not contain metal ion containing motifs which would render doravirine susceptible to chelation. Therefore, doravirine is not likely to have a clinical meaningful interaction with cation-containing antacids or acid reducing agents. The study results support this hypothesis.
L2098: **Conclusion:** Administration of doravirine with an antacid oral suspension or pantoprazole does not have a clinically meaningful effect on the pharmacokinetics of doravirine.

L2136: ## **Protocol P012:** A Study to Assess the Effects of Multiple Oral Doses of MK-1439 on the Single-Dose Pharmacokinetics of an Oral Contraceptive (Ethinyl Estradiol and Levonorgestrel) in Healthy Adult Female Subjects
L2137: **Study Design:** This was an open-label, 2-period, fixed-sequence study. Twenty (20) healthy, non-tobacco using, postmenopausal or oophorectomized, adult female subjects were enrolled in the study. In Period 1 (Treatment A), subjects received a single oral dose of Nordette®-28 following an overnight fast, followed by pharmacokinetic sampling for up to 96 hours postdose. There was a washout of at least 7 days between dosing in Period 1 and the first dose in Period 2. In Period 2, oral doses of MK-1439 were administered QD for 17 consecutive days, with a single dose of Nordette®-28 coadministered on Day 14 following an overnight fast, followed by pharmacokinetic sampling for up to 96 hours postdose.

L2148: ## 15.5.3. Intrinsic Factor PK Studies
L2149: ## Protocol P09: A Relative Bioavailability Study to Compare the Pharmacokinetics of MK-1439 in Male and Female Subjects and Healthy Elderly and Young Adult Subjects
L2150: **Study Design:** This was an open-label, 1-period, parallel-group study in healthy elderly male and female subjects and healthy young female subjects to identify gender- and age-related difference in the pharmacokinetics of MK-1439. Thirty-six (36) subjects, 12 subjects per group, were enrolled in the study. Group 1 consisted of healthy elderly male subjects, aged between 65 and 80 years (inclusive), Group 2 consisted of healthy elderly female subjects, aged between 65 and 80 years (inclusive), and Group 3 consisted of healthy young female subjects, aged between 18 and 50 years (inclusive). Subjects received a single oral 100-mg dose of MK-1439 (one Phase I tablet) with 240 mL of water after fasting overnight for at least 8 hours.

L2154: **Bioanalytical Method Assessment:** The standard curve and QC data indicated that the plasma assay methods for MK-1439 were precise and accurate. All samples were analyzed in the timeframe supported by frozen stability storage data.

L2169: ## **Protocol P019: An Open-Label, Single-Dose Study to Investigate the Influence of Hepatic Insufficiency on the Pharmacokinetics of MK-1439**
L2170: **Study Design:** This was a non-randomized, open-label, single-dose study to compare the pharmacokinetics of MK-1439 in subjects with moderate hepatic insufficiency (a score of 7 to 9 on the Child-Pugh scale) to healthy matched control subjects. Eight (8) subjects with moderate hepatic insufficiency and 8 healthy matched control subjects were enrolled. MK-1439 was administered following an overnight fast.

L2172: Bulk Product Description	Manufacturing Lot Number

L2173: **PK Sampling:** Blood samples for determination of plasma MK-1439 concentrations were collected at pre-dose and at 0.5, 1, 1.5, 2, 3, 6, 12, 24, 48 and 72 hours post-dose.

L2177: **Bioanalytical Method Assessment:** The standard curve and QC data indicated that the plasma assay methods for MK-1439 were precise and accurate. All samples were analyzed in the timeframe supported by frozen stability storage data.
L2178: **Conclusion:** The administration of a single dose of 100 mg MK-1439 to subjects with moderate hepatic insufficiency results in similar exposure to that in healthy matched control subjects, indicating that moderate hepatic insufficiency does not affect the pharmacokinetics of MK-1439.
L2179: ## **Protocol 051: An Open-Label, Single-Dose Study to Evaluate the Pharmacokinetics of MK-1439 (Doravirine) in Subjects with Severe Renal Impairment**
L2180: **Objectives:** To evaluate the plasma PK of a single oral dose of 100 mg doravirine in subjects with severe renal impairment (baseline estimated glomerular filtration rate [eGFR] &lt; 30 mL/min/1.73 m2) compared to healthy matched control subjects.
L2181: **Study Design:** This was an open-label, single-dose, renal impairment study to compare the pharmacokinetics (PK) of doravirine in subjects with severe renal impairment to that in healthy mean matched control subjects [control subjects were matched to the mean age (\pm 10 years) and weight (\pm 10 kg) of subjects with renal impairment]. Sixteen (16) adult, male and female subjects were enrolled, 8 with severe renal impairment and 8 healthy matched control subjects. On Day 1, subjects received a single oral dose of 100 mg doravirine following an overnight fast.
L2182: ## **Formulation:**
L2183: Bulk Product Description	Manufacturing Lot Number
FCT MK-1439 (005F) 100 mg	WL00059370
L2184: **PK Sampling:** Blood samples for determination of plasma MK-1439 concentrations were collected at pre-dose and at 0.5, 1, 2, 3, 4, 6, 12, 24, 48, 72 and 96 hours (for severe renal impairment subjects only) post-dose.

L2190: ## 15.5.4. Pharmacometrics Review
L2191: ## Population PK Analysis
L2192: The goal of this population PK analysis (popPK) was to develop a population pharmacokinetic (PK) model to assess sources of variability (intrinsic and extrinsic covariates) of doravirine (DOR) in healthy subjects and HIV-1 infected subjects.
L2193: The population PK model included 20 Phase 1 trials, 1 Phase 2b trial, and 2 Phase 3 trials, comprising 341 healthy subjects and 959 HIV-1 infected subjects receiving oral doses of DOR as a single entity or as DOR/3TC/TDF. The PK of DOR was characterized with a one-compartment model described by first-order absorption (K_a), volume of distribution (V/F), and linear clearance (CL/F) from the central compartment. The less than dose-proportional increases in DOR exposure were described by estimation of two relative bioavailability (F_1) for the dose ranges of &lt;30 mg and &gt; 120 mg in comparison to the dose range of 30-120 mg. An additive residual error model was employed for log-transformed data. Separate residual errors were estimated for the Phase 1 data versus the Phase 2b/3 data, and within the Phase 1 data for the first 0.5 h post-dose versus the remaining time points. Parameter estimates of final model with significant covariates were provided in Table 120.
L2194: No signs of model misspecification were identified in the goodness-of-fit plots (Figure 20). Prediction-corrected visual predictive check showed that the final model adequately described the observed PK profile of DOR in both healthy subjects and HIV-infected patients (Figure 21). Bootstrap analyses demonstrated consistency in parameter estimates and indicated the robustness of the model (Figure 22).
L2195: Automated stepwise covariate model building (SCM) was applied to select significant covariates. The final population PK model included statistically significant effects of body weight and HIV-1 infection status on apparent volume of distribution and age on apparent clearance. In addition, the effects of all evaluated covariates on the DOR PK parameters were illustrated in the forest plot based on 1000 bootstrap results of full covariate model (Figure 24). The effects of continuous covariates on PK parameters were illustrated though comparing the PK parameter in a patient with extreme (5th or 5th percentile) covariate value relative to the parameter in a patient with the median covariate value. Overall the effects of these covariates on PK parameters were modest. Even statistically significant covariates age, body weight and healthy status were not expected to result in a greater than 25% change in DOR PK parameters. PopPK analysis indicated no significant effect of concomitant administration of strong CYP3A4 inhibitors on CL (Figure 24). However, this analysis was limited by the very small number of subjects who received strong CYP3A4 inhibitors (N=8) in the Phase 2/3 trials.
L2196: AUC0-24, Cmax, and C24 at steady-state were simulated from posthoc compartmental parameter estimates of final model for each subject in the Phase 2b and 3 trials. The summary statistics of these PK parameters by dose and by study were summarized in Table 121.
L2197: ## NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation – NDA 210806 and NDA 210807
## Doravirine (DOR) and DOR/3TC/DOR
L2198: The absolute bioavailability of the final market image MK-1439 film-coated tablet was also estimated via a population PK model. The analysis was based on PK data collected from 24 healthy fasted subjects receiving a single oral (PO) dose of 100 mg final market image FCT MK-1439 from Phase 1 trial P039 and 11 healthy subjects receiving a single IV infusion of 100 mcg MK-1439 from Phase 1 trial P044. A two-compartment model with first order absorption was used to fit the data. Between-subject variability on CL and V was described by exponential error models and residual variability was captured by a proportional model. No covariate was included in this analysis as the focus was to estimate absolute BA. The final PK model adequately described the concentration-time profiles of MK-1439 in the studied dataset (Figure 24). Parameter estimates of the final model are presented in Table 3. Based on this analysis, the absolute bioavailability of the 100 mg final market image tablet of MK-1439 is estimated to be 64%, indicating that MK-1439 has moderate bioavailability.

L2220: ## Dose/Exposure Response Relationships
L2221: Relationships between DOR dose or exposure and efficacy and safety were characterized based on 1 phase 2b trial (P007) and 2 Phase 3 trials (P018 and P021). The Phase 2b trial (P007) was a dose ranging trial in treatment-naïve HIV-1 infected subjects where doses of 25, 50, 100, and 200 mg
L2222: ## NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation – NDA 210806 and NDA 210807
## Doravirine (DOR) and DOR/3TC/DOR
L2223: QD were evaluated with DOR. Two Phase 3 trials were conducted in treatment-naïve HIV-1 infected subjects, both with DOR at 100 mg QD doses.
L2224: The dose/exposure response evaluations consisted of 947 HIV-1 infected subjects receiving oral doses of DOR as a single entity or as DOR/3TC/TDF. The efficacy evaluations were assessed for the proportion of subjects achieving HIV-1 RNA levels at two cut-offs: &lt;50 copies/mL, and &lt;40 copies/mL. Both were assessed using the snapshot and observed failure analysis approaches. Additionally, occurrence of protocol defined virologic failure (PDVF) was included as an efficacy endpoint in the evaluation. The relationship between DOR exposure and efficacy endpoints were evaluated in Phase 2 and Phase 3 studies separately due to different definition of PDVF used between Phase 2 and Phase 3 studies. The safety endpoints used in the analysis included neuropsychiatric adverse events through week 8 and 48, and lipid profiles (fasting LDL-C and fasting non-HDL-C) at week 48. Depending on the safety and efficacy endpoints, different trials were pooled in the analysis. A summary of the number of subjects included in the analyses for each of the response variables per study was provided in Table 123.
L2225: Dose/exposure response relationships for efficacy endpoints and neuropsychiatric AEs were performed using logistic regression. The evaluations of lipid profiles were conducted with linear regression analysis.
L2226: Individual estimates of AUC0-24, C24 and Cmax at steady state were obtained based on posthoc PK parameters from final popPK model. Steady state C24 and AUC0-24 were considered primary exposure metrics for the efficacy PK/PD analysis. Cmax and AUC0-24 were considered primary exposure metrics for the safety PK/PD analysis. The potential confounding effects of baseline risk factors on efficacy or safety endpoints were explored. A full model approach was first applied to incorporate all the potential baseline risk factors. The covariates with p-value larger than 0.05 were removed from the full model after backward elimination. The risk factors included in the full model include: age, weight, race, sex, screening HIV-1 RNA.

L2240: ## Dose Response for Safety

L2242: The parameter estimates of final D-R model for neuropsychiatric AEs are provided in Table 125. No statistically significant differences in incidence rates was found among 25 mg, 100 mg and 200 mg.
L2243: ## NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation – NDA 210806 and NDA 210807
## Doravirine (DOR) and DOR/3TC/DOR
L2244: A higher incidence rate of neuropsychiatric AEs was found in 50 mg compared to 100mg. However, this was likely due to chance because of small sample size as no difference in incidences rate was found when comparing the higher dose 200 mg to 100 mg.

L2258: ## Exposure Response for Efficacy
L2259: Phase 2b Trial (P007): The observed proportion of subjects achieving HIV-1 RNA levels at two cut-offs: &lt;50 copies/mL, and &lt;40 copies/mL and occurrence of protocol defined virologic failure stratified by DOR exposure quartiles in the phase 2b study were shown in Figure 28. The proportion of response or virologic failure appeared to be similar across the 4 exposure quartiles. A summary of the logistic regression results investigating a linear relationship between the five efficacy endpoints and the DOR AUC0-24 and C24 in the Phase 2b trial is provided in Table 127. Log-transformed baseline viral load was the only significant predictor included in the analysis. The distribution of the background anti-viral medication is assumed to be balanced across DOR exposure quartiles. The exposure-response relationships between DOR PK (AUC and C24) and all efficacy endpoints were flat over the range of exposure achieved over the 25 to 200 mg doses, as no slope of DOR PK on efficacy variables were significantly different from zero. A visualization of the predicted exposure-response relationships for each of the four HIV-1 RNA efficacy endpoints and virologic failure based on final models with DOR steady state PK (C24 and AUC0-24) was presented in Figure 29 and Figure 30. Consistent with the nonsignificant slope estimates presented above, no trends were observed between response and DOR steady-state C24 or AUC0-24 over the range of exposures achieved over the 25 to 200 mg QD doses.

L2270: ## Phase 3 Trials (P018 &amp; P021)
L2271: The observed proportion of subjects achieving HIV-1 RNA levels at two cut-offs: &lt;50 copies/mL and &lt;40 copies/mL and occurrence of protocol defined virologic failure stratified by 10 DOR exposure quartiles in the phase 3 studies are shown in Figure 31. A trend of lower response and higher virologic failure rates were observed in the lowest exposure quartile.
L2272: A summary of the exposure-response slope estimates for each of the endpoint-exposure parameter combinations is provided in Table 128. Log-transformed baseline viral load and age were the significant predictors included in the analysis. Statistically significant exposure-response relationships were identified between DOR PK (AUC and C24) and nearly all evaluated efficacy endpoints over the exposures achieved at the 100 mg QD dose. A visualization of the predicted exposure-response relationships for each of the four HIV-1 RNA efficacy endpoints and virologic failure based on final models with DOR steady state PK (C24 and AUC0-24) was presented in Figure 32 and Figure 33. Although the slopes were statistically different from zero, the ER relationships were generally flat with a minimal decrease in efficacy in subjects with low DOR exposure. Even in the lowest exposure quartile, more than 80% response rate and only about 10% virologic failure were predicted.

L2294: ## Lipid Profiles
L2295: Distribution of change in LDL-C and non-HDL-C from baseline at week 48 by DOR exposure quartiles is provided in Figure 36. The change in lipid profiles from baseline appears similar across DOR exposure quartiles.
L2296: The relationships between DOR PK and change in LDL-C and non-HDL-C from baseline were assessed with linear regression models. The parameter estimates for individual final models are presented in Table 130. A positive trend of exposure-response relationship was observed between DOR PK and change in LDL-C from baseline at week 48, although the relationship was not statistically significant. For non-HDL-C, a significant non-zero slope of DOR PK on change from baseline was detected. Predicted relationships between DOR PK and change in lipids based on final model (Figure 37) indicated that lower DOR exposure was associated with slightly larger decreases in lipids from baseline. The predicted change in lipids from baseline at 5th and 95th percentiles of DOR exposures at a dose of 100 mg QD is presented in Table 131.
L2297: An exploratory analysis was also conducted to investigate whether concomitant administration with a strong CYP3A4 inhibitor would affect the safety profile. The incidence rates of neuropsychiatric AE and change in lipid profiles from baseline were compared between patients who took strong CYP3A4 inhibitors and those who did not. Among 5 patients who took strong CYP3A4 inhibitors during treatment in study P007 and P021, 3 patients experienced neuropsychiatric AE. Although this point estimate was higher compared to 31% of neuropsychiatric AE in patients without concomitant medication of strong CYP3A4 inhibitor, the confidence intervals overlapped because of the small sample size. Similarly, although the median decrease in LDL-C and non-HDL-C at week 48 from baseline in 9 patients with concomitant medication of strong CYP3A4 inhibitors was smaller compared to the median decrease in all other patients who did not, this difference is not statistically significant. Both comparisons were limited by the very small number of subjects who took strong CYP3A4 inhibitors for at least 7 days during treatment in the phase 2b/3 trial.

L2312: ## 15.5.5. PBPK Review
L2313: ## **Study Objectives:**
L2314: - To develop and qualify a PBPK model for doravirine (DOR);
- To simulate DDIs with the CYP3A inhibitors;
- To simulate DDIs with CYP3A inducers;
- To develop and qualify a PBPK model for the metabolite of DOR, M9
L2315: **Executive Summary:** The applicant has developed a physiologically-based pharmacokinetic (PBPK) model for DOR using SimCYP® version 16 (v16). The model was used to predict drug-drug interactions (DDIs) with moderate CYP3A inhibitors diltiazem (classified as a strong CYP3A inhibitor in the FDA DDI website) and verapamil to inform dose recommendations for moderate CYP3A inhibitors.
L2316: The DOR PBPK model was built with physicochemical and in vitro data, clinical IV data, single oral dose PK data, and human absorption, metabolism, excretion (AME) data. Clearance was parameterized using clinical IV data, with the majority of the elimination attributed to CYP3A4-mediated metabolism, after accounting for the minor amount of renal clearance observed in clinical PK studies. Absorption was modeled using single oral dose PK data and in vitro permeability data. Distribution was modeled as one-compartment, with the steady state volume of distribution (Vss) obtained from clinical IV data. Simulated concentration-time profiles with the DOR model closely followed observed concentration-time data for a single 100 mg DOR oral dose (study P039) and a 100 µg DOR IV dose (study P044) with simulated AUC_{0-\infty} and oral Cmax values within 10% of those observed.
L2317: The DOR PBPK model was further verified by comparison of simulated and observed AUC, Cmax, and C24 ratios for drug-drug interactions with ketoconazole (strong CYP3A inhibitor) (study P010), rifampin (strong CYP3A inducer) (study P011), and efavirenz (moderate CYP3A inducer; DOR plasma concentrations following cessation of efavirenz therapy) (study P020). The model can accurately simulate the observed effect of ketoconazole (SimCyp built-in model), with the increase in DOR AUC and Cmax within 10% of the observed values. The effect of efavirenz (SimCyp built-in model) pretreatment on AUC and Cmax was also predicted within 30% of observed. However, the impact of co-administration of rifampin (SimCyp built-in model) on AUC and Cmax was under-predicted by 2-fold, although the model did capture a significant decrease in exposure caused by rifampin.
L2318: To evaluate whether multiple strong CYP3A inhibitors would further increase DOR concentrations, SimCyp built-in ritonavir (a CYP3A strong inhibitor) model was utilized by the reviewer to predict the effect of ritonavir on DOR concentrations and to determine if the combination of ritonavir and ketoconazole would have a greater effect on DOR concentrations as compared to ritonavir or ketoconazole alone. The analysis indicates that adding a strong CYP3A inhibitor on top of another strong CYP3A inhibitor would not further significantly increase DOR concentrations.
L2319: The reviewer conducted a sensitivity analysis on the maximal induction effect (Ind_{max}) of rifampin, and observed that increasing the Ind_{max} from 16-fold to 40-fold for rifampin improved the prediction of the effect of rifampin on DOR to within 20% of the observed values. In addition, the reviewer also evaluated the predicted effect of rifabutin on DOR as compared to the observed data (study P035) by using an FDA in-house rifabutin PBPK model. The sensitivity analysis was also conducted for Ind_{slope} of rifabutin. Modifying the Ind_{slope} value of rifabutin from 35 to 15 improved R_{predicted/observed} for AUC (predicted geometric mean exposure ratio/observed geometric mean exposure ratio) from 0.7 to 1.04 and improved R_{predicted/observed} for C_{max} from 0.76 to 0.85. Furthermore, the reviewer simulated the effect of cessation periods following rifampin or rifabutin treatment prior to the initiation of DOR treatment to determine the appropriate period of time to allow for the CYP3A inductive effect to subside. The analysis indicates that 3-4 weeks of stopping rifampin and 2-3 weeks of stopping rifabutin prior to DOR treatment would be appropriate.
L2320: The applicant also simulated the effect of the moderate CYP3A inhibitors, diltiazem and verapamil on DOR exposure at clinically relevant dose regimens. However, because there are sufficient clinical safety data available for coadministration of DOR and a strong CYP3A inhibitor (ketoconazole), analysis results with the moderate CYP3A inhibitors will not be described in the review.
L2321: During the review, the reviewer raised a concern over the potential for significantly increased M9 concentrations (DOR major metabolite) when DOR is coadministered with a CYP3A inducer. The applicant then added the M9 model to the PBPK analysis to evaluate the effect of a CYP3A inducer on M9 concentrations. Although there is no human PK data for M9 to verify the effect, the model is generally reasonable. The analysis shows that a strong CYP3A inducer may increase M9 AUC and C_{max} by up to 2-fold and 3-fold, respectively. By increasing DOR dosing frequency from once daily to twice daily when coadministered with a moderate CYP3A inducer, rifabutin, M9 AUC is expected to increase approximately 4-fold, at maximum.

L2344: ## PBPK Modeling for Doravirine:
L2345: The modeling workflow for the DOR PBPK model is comprised of model building, model verification, and model application. Physicochemical and in vitro data, clinical IV and single oral dose PK data, and human AME data were used to build the model. Simulated and observed AUC and Cmax values following IV and oral administration were compared to ensure the model adequately captured the PK parameters of DOR. Simulated C24 values were also compared to those observed for the oral dose. In addition, simulated concentration-time profiles were overlaid with observed data to assess, by visual inspection, the ability of the model to describe the central tendency and shape of the observed concentration-time profiles. Model was verified by comparing simulated and observed AUC, Cmax, and C24 ratios for DDIs with several CYP3A modulators: ketoconazole (strong CYP3A inhibitor) (P010), rifampin (strong CYP3A inducer) (P011), and efavirenz (moderate CYP3A inducer; DOR plasma concentrations during CYP3A induction washout following cessation of efavirenz therapy) (P020).
L2346: A summary of the DOR PBPK model input parameters is shown in Table 135. Intrinsic clearance was calculated with the observed human IV clearance using the retrograde model within SimCYP®. The majority of the elimination was attributed to CYP3A4-mediated metabolism, after accounting for the minor amount of renal clearance observed in clinical PK studies. Absorption was modeled using SimCYP®'s First-Order Absorption Model, with the fraction absorbed (Fa) estimated from clinical data and the absorption rate constant (ka) predicted from in vitro permeability data. Distribution was modeled as one-compartment using the Minimal PBPK model, with the steady state volume of distribution obtained from clinical IV data.
L2347: The following assumptions were made in the development of the DOR PBPK model and they are generally reasonable:
L2348: - The hepatic clearance is mediated solely via CYP3A4;
- The free fraction in the gut is 1.0;
- The impact of P-gp on absorption and clearance is negligible

L2356: ## **PBPK Modeling for M9:**
L2357: The applicant has incorporated M9 into the DOR PBPK model to evaluate the effect of rifabutin on the M9 concentrations, with the following assumptions:
L2358: ## NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation – NDA 210806 and NDA 210807
## Doravirine (DOR) and DOR/3TC/DOR
L2359: - **Formation rate of M9:** Based on the mass balance study (P008, hAME), 55% of the absorbed dose of DOR is excreted as M9, 12.9% is recovered in urine as DOR, and any remaining DOR is converted to other metabolites. As a result, the rate of M9 formation via CYP3A4 was specified as 55% of the total clearance of DOR, corresponding to 65% of the hepatic clearance. As DOR is primarily metabolized by CYP3A4, the remaining 35% of hepatic clearance was assigned to CYP3A4 metabolism (inducible) that does not lead to the formation of M9.
- **Distribution of M9:** The volume of distribution of M9 was assumed to be one compartment and a minimal PBPK model was used. M9 Vss was predicted from the plasma free fraction, determined in vitro, and the predicted pKa and logP.
- **Elimination of M9:** Based on hAME data, M9 is primarily cleared intact in urine and feces, with a minor amount converted to secondary metabolites. Therefore, M9 elimination was parameterized as renal clearance and additional systemic clearance to represent the excretion in feces, with 10% assigned to non-inducible metabolism. In the absence of plasma concentration-time data for M9 in humans, the total clearance in the model was fit to match the M9/DOR AUC ratio observed in the hAME trial.
L2360: Table 136 summarizes M9 PBPK model input parameters.

L2367: ## **Doravirine Model:**
L2368: Simulated and observed concentration-time profiles for intravenous infusion of 100 \mug doravirine over 15 minutes (P044) and single oral administration of 100 mg of the final market image film-coated tablet (P039) are displayed in Figure 40. The simulated concentration-time profiles closely followed observed concentration-time data, except for the Cmax following the IV dose.
L2369: **Figure 37: Simulated and Observed Concentration-Time Profile (a) Following a Single 15 Minute Intravenous Infusion of 100 \mug DOR and (b) Following a Single Oral Dose of 100 mg DOR**
L2370: Simulated and observed geometric mean AUC_{0-\infty}, Cmax, and C24 values are shown in Table 137. Simulated AUC_{0-\infty} and Cmax values were within 10% of the observed values, and C24 was ~35% lower than observed for the 100 mg oral dose. Cmax for the 100 µg IV dose was underpredicted by ~50% because a one compartment minimal PBPK model was used to characterize the PK of DOR based on the observed monophasic behavior following oral dosing. However, following administration of the IV microdose, a biphasic decline was observed with the inflection between alpha and beta phases occurring prior to 2 hours post-dose. This biphasicity was not observed following oral dosing and thus, the simulated oral profile captured the observed concentration-time data well.

L2373: ## **DDI model:**
L2374: Simulation of DOR exposure following coadministration of DOR with ketoconazole (strong CYP3A inhibitor), rifampin (strong CYP3A inducer), and efavirenz (moderate CYP3A inducer; DOR plasma concentrations during CYP3A induction washout following cessation of efavirenz therapy) were conducted by the applicant. The simulated GMRs for AUC, Cmax and C24 of DOR were compared with the observed data from the DDI studies. As shown in Table 138, the model captured the effect of ketoconazole well (P010), with the increase in DOR AUC and Cmax within 10% of observed values. The simulations also sufficiently recovered the effect of CYP3A induction by efavirenz (P020), with the decrease in AUC and Cmax within 30% of the observed values on both Day 15 and Day 28, following 14 days of efavirenz therapy. The impact of co-
L2375: administration of rifampin (P011) on DOR AUC and Cmax was under-predicted by ~2-fold, shown as the ratio of the predicted vs observed (R_{\text{predicted/observed}}) in Table 138.

L2378: ## **Effect of CYP3A Inducers on DOR and M9 Exposures**
L2379: Under-prediction of the effect of rifampin with SimCyp has been reported. Analysis has been conducted previously by the FDA (Wagner, et al., Clin Pharmacokinetics, 55:475-483, 2016) which showed that improved predictions were obtained when the rifampin model was modified by increasing the induction potency of CYP3A (maximal induction, Indmax). Sensitivity analysis for CYP3A Indmax by rifampin was conducted by the reviewer (Figure 41) and the analysis shows that AUC ratio and Cmax ratio decreased with increasing Indmax, thus higher Indmax is more closely approaching the observed ratios of 0.12 and 0.43, respectively. Increasing Indmax from 16 (SimCyp default value) to 40 improved R_{\text{predicted/observed}} from 2.17 to 1.08 for AUC and from 1.65 to 1.12 for Cmax (Table 139).
L2380: ## NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation – NDA 210806 and NDA 210807
## Doravirine (DOR) and DOR/3TC/DOR
L2381: To evaluate whether change of Ind_{max} value for rifampin affects the prediction of rifampin effect on other CYP3A substrates, midazolam from SimCyp®'s v16 library compound file was used as a substrate. As shown in Table 140, with Ind_{max} of 40 for rifampin, the prediction of midazolam exposure reduction by rifampin is more accurately predicted as compared to Ind_{max} of 16 as defined in the SimCyp® library.
L2382: The effect of the number of days of rifampin therapy (600 mg QD) cessation prior to initiation of DOR therapy on DOR concentrations was evaluated with Ind_{max} of 16 and 40. As shown in Figure 42, following 21 days (3 weeks) and 28 days (4 weeks) of rifampin cessation, doravirine AUC ratio is reduced to 0.13, by assigning Ind_{max} to 16 and 40, respectively. Because rifampin 900 mg QD is used off-label by physicians, the period of rifampin cessation required prior to initiation of DOR therapy following rifampin 900 mg QD for 4 weeks was also evaluated by the reviewer as a maximum effect. The analysis shows that increasing the rifampin dose from 600 mg QD to 900 QD will reduce DOR AUC to 25% (19%-31%) following 4 weeks of rifampin cessation. The magnitude of the DOR AUC reduction is acceptable based on the Phase 2 data.
L2383: The updated model with M9 incorporation into the DOR PBPK model was evaluated using rifampin's CYP3A Ind_{max} of 40 on parent drug, and results of the analysis are shown in Table 141.
L2384: Sensitivity analysis on the following 2 scenarios were conducted by the applicant:
L2385: - All of the hepatic CL that forms the non-M9 metabolites (35% of hepatic CL) is specified as an undefined, non-inducible, hepatic CL rather than inducible CYP3A4 metabolism;
- M9 elimination was parameterized as renal clearance with additional systemic clearance to represent the excretion in feces, and 10% assigned to non-inducible metabolism.
L2386: In Scenario 1, M9 AUC ratio is projected to be higher relative to the base case (1.70-fold increase versus 1.20-fold increase in M9 for the base case for rifampin) because the component of hepatic clearance forming the additional minor metabolites is not inducible. In Scenario 2, a 0.61-fold change in M9 exposure is projected following coadministration with rifampin, representing a ~50% decrease from the base case simulation.
L2387: The analysis shows that a strong CYP3A inducer may increase M9 AUC and C_{max} by up to 2-fold and 4-fold, respectively. By increasing DOR dosing frequency from once daily to twice daily when coadministered with a moderate CYP3A inducer, rifabutin, M9 AUC is expected to increase approximately 4-fold, at maximum.

## Doravirine (DOR) and DOR/3TC/DOR

L2399: Figure 39: Doravirine AUC Reduction Following Days of Cessation of Rifampin Using Indmax of 16 and 40
L2400: The effect of rifabutin on DOR concentrations was evaluated by the Reviewer using the FDA's in-house compound profile. Rifabutin CYP3A Ind_{slope} (induction slope) was also evaluated by the sensitivity analysis. The results of the analysis (Figure 43) indicate that DOR AUC ratio and Cmax ratio decreased with increasing rifabutin CYP3A Ind_{slope}. Using the in-house rifabutin Ind_{slope} of 35, the effect of rifabutin on DOR is under-estimated (predicted DOR AUC ratio of 0.35 and Cmax ratio of 0.75 vs observed AUC ratio of 0.50 and Cmax ratio of 0.99), while with the Ind_{slope} of 15, the predicted DOR AUC ratio (0.52) and Cmax ratio (0.84) are more similar to the observed. By modifying Ind_{slope} of rifabutin from 35 to 15 improved R_{predicted/observed} for AUC (predicted geometric mean exposure (AUC or Cmax) ratio/observed geometric mean exposure ratio) from 0.7 to 1.04 and R_{predicted/observed} for Cmax from 0.76 to 0.85.
L2401: Figure 40: Sensitivity Analysis for CYP3A Ind_{slope} by Rifabutin
L2402: The effect of days of cessation following rifabutin treatment prior to DOR therapy on DOR concentrations was evaluated with Ind_{slope} of 15. As shown in Figure 44, following 14 days (2 weeks) and 21 days (3 weeks) of rifabutin cessation, the reduction in doravirine AUC is 13% and 7%, respectively.
L2403: **Figure 41: Doravirine AUC Reduction Following Days of Cessation of Rifabutin**
L2404: ## Effect of Ritonavir or Combination of Ritonavir and Ketoconazole
L2405: The effect of ritonavir or the combination of ritonavir and ketoconazole on DOR was evaluated by the Reviewer using SimCyp's library compound profiles. As shown in Table 142, R_{\text{predicted}/\text{observed}} for AUC ratio (AUCR) and C_{\text{max}} ratio (C_{\text{max}}R) are 1.23 and 0.89, respectively, for the effect of ritonavir on DOR. The combination of ritonavir and ketoconazole with DOR only increased the AUC ratio (predicted/observed) of DOR and C_{\text{max}} ratio to 1.17 and 1.02, respectively, as compared to coadministration of DOR with ritonavir (Table 143). Therefore, it is not expected that adding a strong CYP3A inhibitor on top of another strong CYP3A inhibitor would further increase DOR concentrations to a clinically relevant extent.

L2411: ## **Reviewer's Conclusions:**
L2412: - Prior to initiation of DOR, 4 weeks of cessation period may be needed following administration of a CYP3A inducer.
- The effect of rifabutin on M9 AUC with doubling DOR dose is expected to be less than 4-fold. Therefore, the proposed increase of DOR dosage from 100 mg QD to 100 mg BID (approximately 12 hours apart) when DOR is coadministered with rifabutin is not expected to pose a safety concern.
- Adding a strong CYP3A inhibitor on top of another strong CYP3A inhibitor would not significantly increase DOR concentrations further. Therefore, no dose adjustment is needed when DOR is coadministered with multiple CYP3A inhibitors.

L2441: ## PHARMACOLOGY/TOXICOLOGY REVIEWDivision of Antiviral Products
L2442: **Date:** March 30, 2018
L2443: **NDA#:** 210806/210807
L2444: **Sponsor:** Merck
L2445: **Indication:** HIV-1
L2446: **Drug name:** Doravirine
L2447: **SDN#:** NDA 210807 SD 26/NDA 210806 SD 27
L2448: **Date of submission:** March 27, 2018
L2449: **Reviewer:** Ilona Bebenek
L2450: **Team Leader:** Hanan Ghantous
L2451: **Submission title:** RESPONSE TO FDA REQUEST FOR INFORMATION RECEIVED
22-MAR-2018
L2452: **Summary:** M9 is the primary metabolite resulting from Doravirine (DOR; MK-1439) via CYP3A metabolism in humans. It is also formed in nonclinical species, at the highest levels in the mouse. An increased level of M9 formation is anticipated with the co-administration of rifabutin (CYP3A inducer). Although nonclinical safety margins for M9 are sufficient based on the three-month mouse GLP study, it was unclear whether M9 was adequately assessed in the AMES assay. DOR was negative in the Ames assay.
L2453: In addition, a QSAR analysis of M9 was conducted by the FDA Chemical Informatics Program. M9 was predicted to be negative for all assays tested. The report is attached at the end of this review.
L2454: **Agency Question:** In your toxicology written summary for NDA 210806, under section 4.1.1 (Microbial Mutagenesis Assay), you stated that "The human metabolite M9 was formed by the liver S-9 activation system". Please provide any details regarding the quantification of M9 in the Ames Assay. Was M9 measured and if so, what were the levels of M9 in the assay?
L2455: **Sponsor Response Summary:** While a quantitative measurement of M9 levels was not performed during conduct of the DOR Ames assay, assessment of levels of the M9 metabolite was performed in separate studies conducted under the conditions of the liver S9 activation system of the Ames assay using radiolabeled DOR. The results of these studies indicated that M9 was generated in micromolar concentrations from DOR after incubation with S9.
L2456: The Ames test was conducted using DOR concentrations of 700 µM to 117,000 µM (117 mM), which corresponds to 30 µg to 5000 µg/plate. Incubations with 900 µM radiolabeled DOR in the S9 Ames activation mixture resulted in concentrations of 10 to 20 µM M9. The concentration tested in the S9 activation system (900 µM) is on the low range of the 700 µM to 117 mM range tested in Ames assay.
L2457: The projected clinical C_{max} concentration of M9 is approximately 0.29 \mu M at the DOR therapeutic dose of 100 mg. Based on the projected C_{max} and the resulting concentrations of M9 in the S9 activation system with 900 \mu M radiolabeled DOR, there is a 34-68X margin (10 \mu M / 0.29 \mu M and 20 \mu M / 0.29 \mu M). Also, it is reasonable to assume that much higher concentrations of M9 were tested in the Ames assay at the top dose of 5000 \mu g / \text{plate} (117 mM). As such, M9 was adequately assessed in the Ames assay.
L2458: To: Ilona Bebenek
cc: Hanan
Ghantous
L2459: From: CDER/OTS/OCP/DARS: The Chemical Informatics Program
L2460: Re: NDA 210806
L2461: Date: April 2, 2018
L2462: The metabolite **M9** of the drug substance doravirine (API) was evaluated by the CDER/OTS/OCP/DARS Chemical Informatics Program for genetic toxicity using (quantitative) structure-activity relationship [(Q)SAR] models. Three software programs were used: *Derek Nexus* 6.0.1 (*DX*), *Leadscope Model Applier* 2.2.2-3 (*LMA*), and *CASE Ultra* 1.6.2.3 (*CU*). To maximize sensitivity and negative predictivity, a positive prediction from any one software program was used to justify a positive overall call for a given endpoint.
L2463: All (Q)SAR model outputs were reviewed with the use of expert knowledge in order to provide additional supportive evidence on the relevance of any positive, negative, conflicting or inconclusive prediction and provide a rationale to support the final conclusion.
L2464: M9
L2465: ## Genetic Toxicity for Predicting the ICH S2 Battery1
L2466: 1 + = positive; - = negative; -* = negative with unclassified features; Eqv = equivocal; NSA = no structural alerts are identified by DX (Derek Nexus cannot differentiate between a negative call and the inability to

L2468: **M9** is predicted to be overall negative for the bacterial mutagenicity, mouse lymphoma, *in vitro* chromosome aberrations, and *in vivo* micronucleus assays based on the application of expert knowledge.
L2469: The equivocal prediction for *Salmonella* mutagenicity by *CU* is based on the presence of an aryl ether fragment. However, the structural alert is shared with the experimental negative API; thus the alert is discounted. *DX* identified no structural alerts in **M9** for *Salmonella* and *E. coli* mutagenicity; however, *DX* notes that it contains an unclassified feature (highlighted in blue below). The unclassified feature was not found in the *Lhasa* Ames test reference set and does not match any structural alerts or examples for (bacterial *in vitro*) mutagenicity in *DX*, leading to a negative prediction. The no calls by *CU* and *LMA* are due to inadequate coverage of the test chemical structural features in the model training sets. Further review of ten additional structurally similar analogs containing the triazolone ring revealed that the unknown fragments are unlikely to cause mutagenicity. Based on the total weight of evidence, **M9** is predicted to be negative for bacterial mutagenicity.
L2470: The positive prediction for the mouse lymphoma assay by *CU* is based on the presence of the urea and chlorobenzene fragments; however, a detailed analysis of the training set compounds and additional analogs revealed that these groups are unlikely to be mutagenic. *DX* identified no structural alerts in **M9** for mouse lymphoma assay. The no call by *LMA* is due to inadequate coverage of the test chemical structural features by the model training set. Furthermore, *CU* identified portions of the triazolone and trifluoromethyl groups as unknown fragments (highlighted in blue below). A structure-based search of supplemental databases yielded seven relevant triazolone analogs that were tested negative for *in vitro* mammalian mutagenesis in mouse lymphoma L5178Y TK+/, Chinese hamster ovary (CHO), or human lymphoid cells. Based on the total weight of evidence, **M9** is predicted to be negative for the mouse lymphoma assay.
L2471: The positive predictions for *in vitro* chromosome aberrations by *CU* are based on the presence of the aryl ether, trifluoromethyl, benzonitrile, dialkylamino, and *N*-acetylurea fragments. The training set compounds behind the *N*-acetylurea alert contain the substructure in a different chemical environment
L2472: than in **M9**, thus the alert is discounted. All other structural alerts are discounted as they are shared with the experimentally negative API. **CU** also identified portions of the *N*-alkyl triazolone moiety as unknown fragments (highlighted in blue below); however, further review of seven additional structurally similar analogs revealed that the unknown fragments are unlikely to cause clastogenicity. Based on the total weight of evidence, **M9** is predicted to be negative for *in vitro* chromosome aberrations.
L2473: The positive prediction for the *in vivo* micronucleus assay by **CU** is based on the aryl ether and chlorobenzene substructures. However, the structural alerts are shared with the experimentally negative API and can be discounted. **CU** also identified portions of the *N*-alkyl triazolone moiety and trifluoromethyl group as unknown fragments (highlighted in blue below). A structure-based search of supplemental databases yielded seven relevant analogs that were experimentally negative for *in vivo* micronucleus in mice and/or rats confirming that these features lack concern. Furthermore, the trifluoromethyl group of **M9** is shared with the experimentally negative of the API. Based on the total weight of evidence, **M9** is predicted to be negative for *in vivo* micronucleus.

L2480: ## PHARMACOLOGY/TOXICOLOGY REVIEWDivision of Antiviral Products
L2481: **Date:** March 30, 2018
L2482: **NDA#:** 210806/210807
L2483: **Sponsor:** Merck
L2484: **Indication:** HIV-1
L2485: **Drug name:** Doravirine
L2486: **SDN#:** NDA 210807 SD 23/NDA 210806 SD 23
L2487: **Date of submission:** March 7, 2018
L2488: **Reviewer:** Ilona Bebenek
L2489: **Team Leader:** Hanan Ghantous
L2490: **Submission title:** RESPONSE TO FDA REQUEST FOR INFORMATION RECEIVED
21-FEB-2018
L2491: **Summary:** M9 is the primary metabolite resulting from Doravirine (DOR; MK-1439) via CYP3A metabolism in humans. It is also formed in nonclinical species, including rat, dog and mouse. At the 100 mg once daily proposed dose of DOR, the safety margins are approximately 2.3X and 2.8X in rats and dogs, based on the NOAELs in chronic toxicology studies and unbound fraction of M9. However, the Sponsor has proposed co-administration of DOR with rifabutin, a CYP3A inducer, which consequently may result in an increased level of M9 formation. As such, although nonclinical safety margins for M9 are sufficient based on the 6- and 9-month rat and dog studies, respectively, it was unclear whether safety margins were adequate for M9 exposures with co-administration of rifabutin. Based on the Clinical Pharmacology estimates, M9 levels may reach a maximum of 43 \muM (for details please see Clinical Pharmacology review for this submission).
L2492: **Agency Question:** We have some concerns over the safety of proposed DOR use with rifabutin. Rifabutin, as a CYP3A inducer, may increase the concentrations of M9, while it reduces doravirine concentrations. The exposure multiples for M9 for rats and dogs at their respective NOAELs relative to humans at doravirine 100 mg QD is estimated to be around 1. During coadministration with rifabutin, M9 concentrations are expected to significantly increase due to the effect of CYP3A induction and the doubling of the daily dose of doravirine. Please provide all available M9 PK and safety data in humans and animals to support the safety of coadministration of DOR and rifabutin.
L2493: **Response Summary:** In a three-month CD-1 mouse GLP toxicology study, the NOAEL was 450 mg/kg/day, corresponding to an AUC of 196 \muM.h/mL. Although M9 levels were not directly measured in this study, DOR and M9 levels were anticipated to be similar in CD-1 mice, based on a mass balance study (1:1 ratio). There were no adverse effects in this study. The Sponsor estimated that the maximum levels of M9 after co-administration with rifabutin will be 19.6 \muM.h/mL. Based on the Clinical Pharmacology estimates, M9 levels may reach a maximum of 43 \muM (for details please see Clinical Pharmacology review for this submission). As such, the exposure margin for M9 is
L2494: between 4.5X and 10X with co-admin of rifabutin (depending on sponsor or Clin Pharm estimate of M9 level after co-admin with rifabutin) and this is considered sufficient.

L2505: **Subject:** Doravirine (NDA 210806/210807)
L2506: ## EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
L2507: The Division of Antiviral Products (DAVP) requested the Division of Applied Regulatory Science (DARS) to perform an evaluation of doravirine, a non-nucleoside reverse transcriptase inhibitor for treatment of HIV. In recent clinical trials where doravirine had been given in combination with other HIV medications, the doravirine arm had been associated with non-significant bilirubin elevations, bile duct stones, and other gallbladder and hepatobiliary events.
L2508: DARS used multiple strategies to evaluate doravirine and other related drugs, including literature review, FAERS case analysis, and chemical structure evaluation. Evaluation of doravirine's metabolism showed that while doravirine is primarily metabolized by CYP3A4 in the liver, it does not significantly inhibit or induce any of the CYPs, nor does it significantly affect several hepatic transporters responsible for biliary events. Case analysis from the FDA Adverse Event Reporting System showed that non-nucleoside reverse transcriptase inhibitors were the least associated with hepatobiliary and gallbladder adverse events compared to protease inhibitors and nucleoside reverse transcriptase inhibitors, and several of the co-medications in doravirine trials were significantly associated with hepatobiliary and gallbladder adverse events. Additionally, chemical structure evaluation demonstrated that while doravirine does not have any features associated with hepatotoxicity, many co-medications given in trials have multiple chemical features associated with a variety of hepatotoxic events.
L2509: In conclusion, DARS could not identify a potential mechanism for doravirine hepatotoxicity. Co-medications given in the trials may be responsible for the observed events.
L2510: ## BACKGROUND
L2511: On January 30, 2018, the Division of Antiviral Products (DAVP) submitted the following consult request to the Division of Applied Regulatory Science (DARS) regarding biliary adverse events and doravirine.
L2512: Original Request: Doravirine (DOR) is a novel non-nucleoside reverse transcriptase inhibitor with an indication of treatment of HIV-1 infection in combination with other antiretroviral agents. NDAs have been submitted for DOR as a single tablet (NDA 210806) and DOR as part of the fixed dose combination tablet also containing lamivudine and tenofovir disoproxil fumarate (DOR/3TC/TDF) (NDA 210807). We have noticed a numerical difference in the number of biliary SAEs in subjects receiving DOR or DOR/3TC/TDF versus subjects receiving the active comparators in the pivotal phase 3 trials (PN018 and PN021) and the supporting phase 2 trial PN007. Specifically in these trials combined, the 5 subjects with biliary SAEs, the 5 subjects with biliary AEs requiring cholecystectomy, and the 6 subjects with moderate or severe biliary AEs all received DOR or DOR/3TC/TDF. Although these rates are low, and the difference is not statistically significant, we find this concerning because there is other evidence that DOR might affect the biliary system. First of all, rates of graded total bilirubin abnormalities were significantly higher in the DOR group versus the comparator groups in PN018 (6.5% versus 1.3%, p=0.0002) and in PN021 (6.6% versus &lt;1%, p\le 0.0001). These graded total bilirubin abnormalities were predominantly not sustained, unconjugated, grade 1 or 2, and not associated with treatment discontinuations or abnormalities in other liver laboratory tests. Of note, only one of the 8 total DOR or DOR/3TC/TDF subjects with a biliary AE had a graded total bilirubin abnormality (one subject with a bile duct stone, who had a single elevated bilirubin level of 12.3 at the time the stone was diagnosed). Secondly, DOR is predominantly metabolized and eliminated by the liver, so it or one of its metabolites may be prone to forming bile stones or otherwise affecting the biliary system. In animal studies, there were no similar findings of biliary AEs.
L2513: Question to be addressed by DARS:
L2514: - Based on its structure and the structure of its metabolites, and its mode of elimination, what are plausible mechanisms whereby DOR might cause biliary AEs?
L2515: ## EVALUATION
L2516: ## Hepatobiliary Adverse Events
L2517: Bilirubin is the oxidative end product of heme catabolism mainly from red blood cells. It is removed from the systemic circulation into the liver through the uptake transporters organic anion transporting protein (OATP) 1B1 and OATP1B3. In hepatocytes, bilirubin is glucuronidated by uridine 5'-diphospho-glucuronosyltransferase (UGT) 1A1. Polymorphisms of UGT1A1 result in different levels of bilirubin glucuronidation and increased systemic bilirubin. Mono- and di-glucuronides are excreted into the bile canaliculi by efflux transporters multidrug resistance protein (MRP) 2 and to a lesser extent by breast cancer resistance protein (BCRP). Some bilirubin
L2518: glucuronides may be carried into the hepatic sinusoid by MRP3 and then taken up again into the hepatocytes by OATP1B1 and OATP1B3 (Figure 1). Drugs may induce hyperbilirubinemia through inhibition of uptake into the hepatocytes, bilirubin conjugation and/or elimination [1].
L2519: Biliary bile salts come from direct hepatic synthesis or recycling through the enterohepatic circulation, which delivers bile salts via the portal vein to the basolateral side of the hepatocyte [2]. Bile salts are taken up into hepatocytes by the sodium taurocholate cotransporting protein (NTCP) and sodium-independent organic anion-transporting polypeptides (OATP1B1, OATP1B3, OATP1A2). The bile salts are then secreted at the apical hepatocellular membrane by the bile salt export pump (BSEP) into the bile canaliculi (Figure 1). Disruption of the function and/or expression of these transporters may lead to the intracellular accumulation of toxic bile acids, resulting in cholestatic liver cell injury [2].
L2520: Figure 1. Bilirubin and Bile Transport
L2521: Clinical trials with doravirine alone and in combination with lamivudine and tenofovir disoproxil fumarate demonstrated a number of biliary adverse events. These included changes in liver enzymes, cholesterol, triglycerides and total bilirubin. Both lamivudine and tenofovir disoproxil fumarate have post-marketing reports of hyperbilirubinemia, and bile duct (choledocholithiasis) and gall bladder (cholelithiasis) stones (Table 1) [3]. However, there is no information in the published literature or product labels [4] that found lamivudine or tenofovir to be inhibitors of the hepatic transporters or UGT1A1.

L2525: Biliary stones are classified by chemical composition as cholesterol (&gt;70% cholesterol), mixed (30% &lt; cholesterol &lt; 70%), and pigmented (cholesterol &lt; 30%) [5]. There are three major routes for the formation of gallstones include cholesterol supersaturation, excess bilirubin, and gallbladder hypomotility or impaired contractility [6]. Cholesterol gallstones are produced primarily because of over secretion of cholesterol by hepatocytes and hypomotility or impaired emptying of the gallbladder [6]. For pigmented gallstones, bilirubin may be present in bile at higher than normal concentrations and then crystallize to form stones [6].
L2526: HIV infection and its subsequent antiretroviral therapy have been connected with alterations in lipid concentrations, including decreased high density lipoprotein cholesterol and increased levels of low density lipoprotein cholesterol, total cholesterol, and triglycerides [7]. Although antiretroviral-naïve HIV-infected patients may present with changed serum lipid levels because the chronic inflammatory effects of HIV infection, there is evidence that antiretroviral treatment exacerbates these effects by augmenting biosynthesis and reducing hepatic clearance of serum lipids [8].
L2527: In a retrospective review of HIV-positive patients who underwent routine abdominal sonography, it was found that the prevalence of cholelithiasis was 12.5% (114/910) [9]. Amongst 680 antiretroviral-experienced patients, the incidence of cholelithiasis was 4.3% [9]. The factors associated with cholelithiasis by a multivariate analysis were exposure to ritonavir-boosted atazanavir for &gt;2 years and older age [9].
L2528: Both atazanavir and indinavir are known to induce cholelithiasis and choledocholithiasis in treated patients (Table 1) [10-14]. The product labels for both drugs list hyperbilirubinemia as an adverse event [4] and they are inhibitors of UGT1A1 and bilirubin hepatic transporters (BSEP, OATP1B1, OATP1B3) [15, 16]. There are two suggested mechanisms for the observed atazanavir-induced cholelithiasis [12]. First, is that precipitation of atazanavir in the bile may enhance the formation of calculi composed of atazanavir and other biliary components [11]. Second, is inhibition of bilirubin conjugation via UGT1A1 that may result in increased bilirubin levels in the bile which could facilitate the formation of gallstones [11].
L2529: ## Doravirine Pharmacokinetics and Metabolism
L2530: *In vitro*, doravirine displayed low intrinsic clearance with oxidation as the predominant hepatic metabolic pathway in preclinical species and humans with renal excretion as a secondary elimination pathway [17]. Using both recombinant enzymes and human liver microsomes with selective anti-CYP antibodies it was found that CYP3A4 plays a major role in doravirine's metabolism [18]. Doravirine was not found to be a reversible inhibitor of major CYP enzymes (CYP1A2, 2B6, 2C8, 2C9, 2C19, 2D6, and 3A4) or UGT1A1 at concentrations up to 100 \muM [18]. Moreover, it was not a time-dependent inhibitor of CYP3A4 [18]. In human hepatocytes, doravirine was not an inducer of CYP1A2 or 2B6, with a weak induction of CYP3A4 (\ge10 \muM) that was not anticipated to have clinical significance [18].
L2531: In cell lines, doravirine was a substrate, but not an inhibitor, of P-glycoprotein (P-gp) [18]. *In vitro*, doravirine inhibited OATP1B1, OATP1B3, OAT1, OAT3, OCT2, and BCRP with IC50 values of 30, 39, 75, 16, 67, and 51 \muM [18], respectively. However, due to the high IC50 values relative to its plasma Cmax of ~2 \muM, it is not anticipated that doravirine would cause clinically relevant interactions with substrates of these transporters [18].
L2532: In clinical trials, doravirine has a Cmax of 1.74-2.88 \muM and AUC0-\infty of 39.5-41.1 hr\times\muM [19, 20]. Drug-drug interaction studies have found that doravirine pharmacokinetics are not affected by dolutegravir or rifampin [21, 22]. Ritonavir, a CYP3A4 inhibitor and inducer, significantly increased doravirine AUC0-\infty and C24hr, with a more modest increase in Cmax [19]. Pretreatment with efavirenz, a CYP3A4 inducer, decreased doravirine's AUC0-\infty, and Cmax [20]. Doravirine slightly decreased the AUC0-\infty, but not Cmax, of midazolam, a probe CYP3A4 substrate [23]. Although not clinically meaningful, atorvastatin Cmax was decreased by 33% while its AUC0-\infty was similar with and without doravirine [24].
L2533: ## FDA Adverse Event Reporting System Analysis
L2534: Class analysis of non-nucleoside reverse transcriptase inhibitors (NNRTIs), nucleoside reverse transcriptase inhibitors (NRTIs), and protease inhibitors (PIs) was performed through a detailed FDA Adverse Event Reporting System (FAERS) analysis using a bioinformatics software program, EFFECTTM by Molecular Health (Heidelberg, Germany). EFFECTTM is currently being evaluated under a Research Collaboration Agreement by the FDA. EFFECTTM integrates the publicly available FAERS data (2004 - 2017 Quarter 2) with various chemical and biological data sources, such as DrugBank, PubChem, and others. This mapping links together drugs, biomolecules, molecular mechanisms, and adverse events. Proportional Reporting Ratios (PRRs), a disproportionality score similar to the Empirical Bayes Geometric Mean (EBGM) in EmpiricaTM Signal, is calculated using the approach described by van Puijenbroek *et al.* [25]. A PRR greater than 1.25 is generally considered a significant association, and a larger PRR score correlates to a more significant signal. For this analysis, the lower bound of the 95% confidence interval of the PRR (PRR05) greater than 1.25 was used in combination with a case count greater than 50.
L2535: Class analysis of reported adverse events (Table 2) showed that NNRTIs were the least associated
L2536: with bile duct and gallbladder adverse events. NRTIs and PIs had a much higher PRR05 score for bile duct stones, cholecystitis acute, cholelithiasis, and elevated bilirubin events comparatively.
L2537: Empirica™ Signal is an internal FDA database built by Oracle (Oracle Corp., Redwood Shores, CA) to access FAERS. FAERS contains data from spontaneous drug adverse event reports from manufacturers, healthcare professionals, lawyers, and consumers, among many others. Case reports include data such as drug(s), indication(s), adverse event(s), outcome, seriousness, and a free-text narrative. To better identify positive associations, a disproportionality score is calculated (EBGM) to show when a drug-adverse event pair is reported more frequently than the background rate [26]. For this case analysis, the lower bound of the 90% confidence interval of the EBGM (EB05) greater than 2.0 was used.
L2538: Individual analysis of reported events for the drugs of interest showed that many of the drugs given in the trials have a strong association with bile duct and gallbladder adverse events (Table 3). There are also noticeable differences between the drugs. For example, a comparison of lamivudine (3TC), tenofovir (TDF) and efavirenz (EFV), drugs that were given in the PN021 DRIVE-AHEAD trial, shows that lamivudine and tenofovir had nearly identical EB05 scores for bile duct stones. The EB05 scores for lamivudine, tenofovir and efavirenz for cholelithiasis were all below 2.0 but the case counts were 129, 54 and 47, respectively. Atazanavir had significant EB05 scores for both bile duct and gallbladder stones. For hyperbilirubinemia, the EB05 scores for lamivudine, tenofovir, and efavirenz were above 3.0. In comparison, both the case counts and EB05 scores for atazanavir (286/37.4) and indinavir (292/6.61) were significant. Therefore, differences between the two groups (DOR/3TC/TDF and EFV/FTC/TDF) in the additional medications may also play a role in adverse events.

L2543: ## Target Prediction
L2544: Two programs that predict molecular targets based on structural similarity to other chemicals were used in this analysis. SEAware by SEACChange Pharmaceuticals can predict binding to 2300 targets throughout the body. SEAware compares the drug of interest's chemical structure to the structures of other drugs and chemicals that are known to bind to each of these targets. A prediction and associated confidence score was generated for each target based on the drug of interest's structural similarity to known binders. Clarity by Chemotargets can predict binding and associated activity (i.e., agonism and antagonism) at 2000 targets throughout the body. Similar to SEAware, Clarity also compares the drug of interest's chemical structure to the structures of other drugs and chemicals that are known to bind to each of these targets using six independent approaches. A prediction and associated confidence score is generated for each target along with potential activity and binding affinity (K_i). For this analysis, the confidence score cutoffs recommended by the respective companies were used, and the strongest predictions for each drug analyzed are provided in Table 4.
L2545: Clarity and SEAware made multiple predictions for the drugs of interest. Of interest, doravirine was predicted to bind to the neuropeptides B/W receptor and inhibit PARP1. No drugs were found that are known to bind to the neuropeptides B/W Type 1 receptor. Animal studies have demonstrated that this receptor may play a role in pain and stress responses [27, 28]. No evidence was found for a role in hepatotoxicity. However, there are currently several approved PARP1 inhibitors (olaparib, rucaparib, and niraparib), which are typically developed to treat cancer. PARP1 inhibition has been shown to attenuate hepatotoxicity induced by other chemicals in animals, including carbon tetrachloride and bromobenzene [29, 30]. Additionally, the three currently approved PARP1 inhibitors are not currently labeled for any gallbladder, biliary, or hepatic adverse events. Therefore, neither predicted secondary target for doravirine appears to be a potential mechanism for induction of hepatotoxicity.

L2549: ## Chemical Structure-Based Analysis
L2550: Doravirine and its metabolite, lamivudine, and tenofovir were evaluated for hepatotoxicity using *Derek Nexus 6.0.1 (DX)* an expert knowledge-based software. In addition, atazanavir, indinavir, and efavirenz were evaluated to identify potential structural attributes that may be responsible for their severe biliary adverse effects.
L2551: A structure-based search was performed using *Leadscope Model Applier 2.2.2-3 (LMA)* for the human adverse hepatotoxicity quantitative structure-activity relationship ((Q)SAR) model training sets [31] to identify chemicals that are structurally similar to doravirine, doravirine metabolite, lamivudine, and tenofovir (Figure 2). The *LMA* models were constructed from a training set of hepatotoxicity data generated from post-market adverse event reports in FDA's Adverse Event Reporting System (FAERS), drug labels, and published literature. The analog tables provided on the subsequent pages list structurally similar analogs to the test chemicals (Figure 3). Training set activity scores from *Leadscope Model Applier* are provided for the endpoints in which the compounds are included in the models. The scores are based predominantly on post-market adverse event reporting.
