L2558: NSA = no structural alerts are identified by *DX* (*DX* cannot differentiate between a negative call and the inability to make a call because of no coverage); + = positive.
L2559: Figure 4: Doravirine and its metabolite
L2560: No structural alerts were reported for doravirine and its metabolite by *DX* (Figure 4). While *DX* did not report any structural alerts, a substructural analysis was performed to identify other potential mechanisms of toxicity. Other drugs with aliphatic nitrile (highlighted in blue), trifluoride (highlighted in orange), and triazolinone and its derivatives (highlighted in red) fragments were reviewed (Figure 4). However, there were not enough data to suggest that these fragments have the potential to cause severe biliary adverse effects or related hepatotoxicity observed in the clinical trials.
L2561: The most structurally similar analog was aprepitant (Appendix A), which has the trifluoride and triazolinone moieties in common. Aprepitant is listed as positive in the LMA liver damage, cholestasis, and bile duct disorders training sets, which is based on FDA's Adverse Event Reporting System. However, there are currently no adverse hepatobiliary effects reported in the FDA label for aprepitant. Aprepitant is given in low doses for short durations and may explain the lack of reported cases. In pre-registration clinical trials, elevated levels of serum aminotransferase occurred in 6% of treated patients compared to 4.3% in controls receiving cancer chemotherapy. The elevated levels were mild to moderate in severity [32]. Due to the lack of supporting evidence, no clear associations between biliary severe adverse effects and the chemical structure of doravirine and its metabolite could be determined at this time.
L2562: Figure 5: Lamivudine
L2563: Chemical structure of Lamivudine (3'-thiacytidine) is shown. The structure features a pyrimidine ring (highlighted in red) attached to a 5-membered ring containing a thioether group (SH). The pyrimidine ring includes an amino group (NH2) and a carbonyl group (C=O). The 5-membered ring is labeled (S) and (R) configurations. The structure is labeled: Lamivudine (Pyrimidine nucleoside alert highlighted in red).
L2564: Lamivudine (Figure 5) is predicted to be positive for human hepatotoxicity by *DX* based on the presence of the pyrimidine nucleoside alert (See Appendix B for analogs). The structural requirements for toxicity of this alert consist of a pyrimidine base bound to a 5-membered ring that resembles the ribose moiety of the natural nucleosides. Natural pyrimidine nucleosides, their deoxy analogs and precursors have been excluded from this alert. According *DX Knowledgebase* 2018, pyrimidine nucleoside analogs cause steatosis with lactic acidosis. The mechanism of hepatotoxicity is based on studies showing that pyrimidine nucleosides disrupt mitochondrial functions by depleting mitochondrial DNA or forming mutated mitochondrial DNA, then impairing mitochondrial functions. In the cases where pyrimidine nucleosides are used in antiretroviral therapy (e.g., zidovudine, stavudine, zalcitabine), elevated levels of alanine aminotransferase were reported and a higher frequency of adverse liver effects was reported in HIV patients with chronic hepatitis. Furthermore, the FDA label of EPIVIR-HBV (lamivudine) supports these findings [4].
L2565: Figure 6: Tenofovir
L2566: Chemical structure of Tenofovir. The organophosphorus group (highlighted in red) is shown attached to the molecule.

Tenofovir
(Organophosphorus alert
highlighted in red)
L2567: Tenofovir (Figure 6) is predicted to be positive for human hepatotoxicity by *DX* based on the presence of an organophosphorus group (See Appendix C for analogs). The structural requirements for toxicity of this alert consist of structural features shared by organophosphates and organophosphonates. *DX Knowledgebase 2018* reports that organophosphates and organophosphonates (e.g., dibutyl phosphate, tris(2-butoxyethyl) phosphate, and diphenyl 2-ethylhexyl phosphate, phosphamidon) have been found to cause mixed injuries in the liver such as hepatocellular necrosis, hypertrophy or perilobular vacuolization of hepatocytes, fatty changes in the liver, steatosis and elevated levels of transaminases and alkaline/acid phosphatases in experimental animals. However, evidence to support these findings in humans is limited.
L2568: Figure 7: Atazanavir
L2569: Chemical structure of Atazanavir. The carbamate group (highlighted in red) is shown attached to the molecule.

Atazanavir
(Carbamate alert highlighted in red)
L2570: Atazanavir (Figure 7) is predicted to positive by *DX*, based on the presence of the carbamic acid groups (See Appendix D for analogs). *DX Knowledgebase 2018* reports that the mechanism is not well understood. However, evidence suggests that human bioactivation and metabolism in the liver to form reactive urethane metabolites may be the cause of liver toxicity. In humans, carbamates have been reported to cause hepatic necrosis.
L2571: Figure 8: Indinavir
L2572: Chemical structure of Indinavir is displayed. The structure features a complex polypeptide backbone with a benzimidazole ring attached to the N-terminus, a central amino acid residue, and a C-terminal residue containing a hydroxyl group and a phenyl ring. The label "Indinavir" is placed below the structure.
L2573: No structural alerts were reported for indinavir (Figure 8) by *DX* (See Appendix E for analogs). However, it should be noted indinavir is slightly structurally similar to atazanavir and its analogs mentioned above.
L2574: Figure 9: Efavirenz
L2575: Chemical structure of Efavirenz is displayed. The structure features a benzene ring substituted with a chlorine atom, attached to a tetrahydrofuran ring. The tetrahydrofuran ring is substituted with a trifluoromethyl group and a carbamate group (highlighted in red). The label "Efavirenz (Carbamate alert highlighted in red)" is placed below the structure.
L2576: Efavirenz (Figure 9) is predicted to be positive by *DX* based on the presence of the carbamate group (See Appendix F for analogs). *DX Knowledgebase 2018* reports that the mechanism is not well understood. However, evidence suggests that human bioactivation and metabolism in the liver to form reactive urethane metabolites may be the cause of liver toxicity. In humans, carbamates have been reported to cause hepatic necrosis.
L2577: ## SUMMARY AND CONCLUSIONS
L2578: In recent clinical trials where doravirine had been given in combination with other HIV medications, the doravirine arm had been associated with non-significant bilirubin elevations, bile duct stones, and other gallbladder and hepatobiliary events. DARS used multiple strategies to evaluate doravirine and other related antiviral drugs. Evaluation of doravirine's metabolism showed that doravirine did not significantly affect most CYPs, P-gp, or several hepatic transporters responsible for hepatobiliary events. Additionally, evaluation of doravirine and related drugs'
L2579: adverse event reports and chemical structures demonstrated that while doravirine itself had a relatively low probability of inducing hepatotoxicity, many co-medications in the trials had a high likelihood of inducing hepatotoxicity. In conclusion, DARS could not identify a potential mechanism for doravirine hepatotoxicity; however, the co-medications given in the trials may be responsible for the observed events.

L2653: Disposition Scheme of DOR in Humans (Figure 36):
Oral Dose leads to Absorbed Drug (~66%) and Unabsorbed Drug (~34%).
Absorbed Drug leads to Systemic Circulation (~96%) and CYP3A-Mediated Metabolism (~4%).
Systemic Circulation leads to CYP3A-Mediated Metabolism (~85%) and Renal Clearance (~15%).

L2660: Diagram illustrating Bilirubin and Bile Transport across the Hepatocyte.
The diagram shows two adjacent hepatocytes separated by the Bile Canaliculi. The left side is labeled "Bile Acid/Salt Transport" and the right side is labeled "Bilirubin Transport".
Bile Acid/Salt Transport (Left Hepatocyte):
NTCP (Sodium Taurocholate Cotransporting Protein) is located on the Basolateral membrane, transporting bile salts into the cell.
OATP1B1, OATP1B3, and OATP1A2 are located on the Basolateral membrane, transporting bile salts into the cell.
BSEP (Bile Salt Export Pump) is located on the Apical membrane, transporting bile salts into the Bile Canaliculi.
MRP3 and MRP4 are located on the Basolateral membrane, transporting bile salts out of the cell into the Sinusoid.
Bilirubin Transport (Right Hepatocyte):
OATP1B1 and OATP1B3 are located on the Basolateral membrane, transporting bilirubin into the cell.
MRP2 is located on the Apical membrane, transporting bilirubin into the Bile Canaliculi.
BCRP (Breast Cancer Resistance Protein) is located on the Apical membrane, transporting bilirubin into the Bile Canaliculi.
MRP3 is located on the Basolateral membrane, transporting bilirubin out of the cell into the Sinusoid.
